Weekly brief

week W39 · 2026-09-26

The technical terms and themes to know this week, what moved in the knowledge graph, and the questions that deserve research. Organised by term, not by source.

This week's terms

model

Claude Opus 5.5

Opus 5.5 is the model Anthropic released on 22 September 2026 with the pitch that it matches Fable 5.1 on quality while costing about 40 percent less on typical workloads and producing output more than 30 percent faster. The pricing is the headline: $4 per million input tokens and $20 per million output, ending four consecutive Opus versions at $5 and $25, with cache reads cut 60 percent on top.

Go deeper →model

GPT-6 Luna

GPT-6 Luna is the low-cost member of the GPT-6 line, sitting beneath Sol and Astra. Its release price is half what its GPT-5.6 predecessor charged after that model's own 80 percent cut in July, and OpenAI has said the reduction is permanent rather than promotional. It is also the only model of the 22 September pair that reaches Free and Go users in the desktop app.

Go deeper →

Themes

  1. Price of the frontier resets, but the bill is set by effort and cache

    Frontier model pricing moved at the top tier: Claude Opus 5.5 launched at $4/$20, 20 percent below the Opus line's long-held $5/$25, with cache reads cut 60 percent; OpenAI halved GPT-6 Sol to $2/$10 and GPT-6 Luna to $0.10/$0.50, while GPT-6 Astra and Fable 5.1 stayed at $10/$50. On Frontier Code the three same-day releases sit within 6 to 7 points at 10 cents, $2 and $4 per task, and Opus 5.5 at medium effort beats max for under a dollar against over five, so Reasoning effort levels and Cache pricing, with over 90 percent of input cached in long agentic runs, decide the invoice. Frontier models also fell from 53 to 45 percent of large-account tokens in a month. Analyst inference: the cuts defend the middle tier, where Cost per task at default effort, not the headline rate, sets Price elasticity.

  2. The harness is now measured, priced and trained

    Four results locate reliability and cost in the layer around the model. Runtime policies applied by the Agent harness at failure-preceding states, with weights and prompt untouched, raise repeated success on Terminal-Bench 2.1 from 64.4 to 73.6 percent while best-of-two moves 1.2 points; truncate-only compaction cuts cost by up to 50 percent; compiling skills into state machines adds 16.1 points and removes 38 to 89 percent of execution tokens, making the Skill library a compiled artefact. An enterprise router recovers 14 to 21 percent of spend in a 10,000-seat emulation by switching models only where the KV cache need not rebuild. Two Qwen-derived systems optimise the harness automatically, one at fixed parameters and interaction budget. Analyst inference: Harness engineering is now a make-or-buy decision with a price attached, Smart model routing is its first line item, and the vendors whose Coding harness enterprises buy are not the ones publishing the gains.

  3. The score belongs partly to the scaffolding

    Independent measurements attribute large shares of Benchmarks results to the setup, not the model. A prompt envelope swings one open system from 0 to 73 percent with weights, cases and scorer fixed, and defining the decision taxonomy lifts every frontier model 24 to 34 points, after which no significant frontier-open difference remains. A benchmark that withholds the question produces a 22x spread where fact recovery spans 1.6x, and some models never reach the puzzle because filters block them, so Refusal behaviour sits inside the measurement. Per-gate governance success of 94.98 percent collapses to 76.92 across a route, and the Opus 5.5 system card's Evaluation awareness split is 0.4 percent in real transcripts against 36 in audits. Analyst inference: Benchmaxxing is the smaller problem; until prompt, harness and refusal variance are held fixed, the 2-to-5 versus 6-to-8 month Open-closed capability gap estimates stay irreconcilable, and Deployment qualification should require a scaffolding ablation.

  4. Self-improvement claims moved from the model to its inputs

    Recursive self-improvement now carries numbers on both sides. A research agent that edits its own code ran autonomously for eight days, accepted seven rewrites and saw Reward hacking fall from 55 to 32 percent without being selected for it. The third-party predeployment evaluation of Claude Opus 5.5 put lab acceleration at about 1.5x, perhaps 30 percent odds of 2x, undated, with no large gain in foresight or feedback-loop construction. Anthropic reports Claude leading 26 percent of its AI R&D with no measured subset fully autonomous. Other loop claims concern Reinforcement learning environments: agents fall from 83.9 to 57.6 percent in noisy environments and recover 15 points when the environment is evolved, a quant system names research path collapse, and a mobile planner runs a human-gated Data flywheel. Analyst inference: this is Lossy self-improvement measured; environment supply and Human oversight at the data gate, not model capability, are the binding inputs.

  5. Agent load rotates the scarce input and pushes containment into the platform

    Agentic inference workloads compile, test and lint, so agent traffic consumes CPUs: spot discounts of up to 90 percent have gone, server lead times run about six months, prices are up 10 to 20 percent, and the data-centre CPU-to-GPU ratio is moving from 1:8 toward 1:4. The cause is Remote sandboxes: agents left laptops for reserved cloud instances, so CPU pricing is planned twelve months out and the Bullwhip effect has passed from GPUs through memory to general compute. At the platform layer, Windows gives agents directory identities and five containment levels, Anthropic's internal agents keep Agent identity across model upgrades, and a GPU-cloud rating finds the hosts beneath frontier models have no immune system. Analyst inference: AI agent containment is moving from lab policy into operating-system and cluster primitives, and Persistent agents make identity, not the sandbox, the audit unit.

Questions to research

  • Does the 20 percent list cut on Claude Opus 5.5 reduce measured Cost per task at default effort on Terminal-Bench and Frontier Code by at least 20 percent once cache-read pricing and effort settings are held at what agentic runs actually use, or is the realised saving dominated by the effort dial? (Research Queue: What a price cut at the frontier tier actually cuts)
  • Does the runtime-policy gain in repeated success (64.4 to 73.6 percent with weights frozen) on Agent reliability reproduce on a model family other than GPT-5.6, and does the HEXIS 38 to 89 percent token reduction hold on a non-Qwen executor? (Research Queue: Runtime interventions versus weights)
  • Do the published 2-to-5 and 6-to-8 month Open-closed capability gap estimates converge when the underlying comparisons are re-run with a fixed prompt envelope and with refusal-blocked runs counted separately, as the Augur and EnigmaForge results imply they should? (Research Queue: What the scaffolding is scoring)
  • Does agent-driven CPU pricing stay 10 to 20 percent above the pre-shortage list, and does the AI data-centre CPU-to-GPU ratio reach 1:4 in any disclosed 2027 cluster design, once the capacity reported as arriving in December comes online? (Research Queue: Tokens per scarce input)
  • Is the 14 to 21 percent spend recovery claimed for Smart model routing at cache-safe switching points net of the classifier's own cost and constrained by any quality measure, and does the finding that the highest-priced model is cheaper on long tool-heavy sessions survive at the post-cut price sheet? (new)

What to watch

  • Frontier model pricing: whether Opus 5.5's $4/$20 and GPT-6 Sol's $2/$10 hold past the launch window, whether the scheduled 25 percent November increase on the GPT-5.6 family arrives, and where Sonnet 5.5 and Haiku 5.5 land against Luna's $0.10/$0.50.
  • Token share versus revenue share: whether the next gateway figures show the frontier tier's share of large-account tokens continuing below 45 percent, which would confirm the middle-tier reading, or recovering after the cuts, which would overturn it.
  • Agent harness: whether the fixed-parameter auto-harnessing claim ships with numbers and whether FIRE's pass^2 gain or CliffCompaction's cost cut is reproduced on a second model family.
  • Recursive self-improvement: whether Anthropic publishes its 26 percent AI R&D leadership share again as a series, whether the elevated-access acceleration estimate gets a stated time period, and whether the eight-day self-rewrite run is replicated with a named base model.
  • Reinforcement learning environments: whether environment evolution shows gains across more than one round, which is the minimum a recursive claim requires, and whether any buyer of RL environments publishes transfer results.
  • CPU pricing: whether spot discounts reappear or lead times fall below six months, either of which would weaken the agent-driven shortage reading.
  • Benchmarks: whether any vendor release table or third-party leaderboard reports a scaffolding ablation or counts refusal-blocked runs separately.
  • Ban Artificial Superintelligence Act: whether the bill, the blocked kill-switch bill and the requested hearings get committee time before the midterms, which would test whether the pacing debate has legislative consequence.

Method

Written after every source folder was refreshed for 2026-W39. Section 1 lists every canonical term whose first_seen is this week, with the first paragraph of its executive explainer; section 3 is computed from the term relations; sections 2, 4 and 5 are the Trend Reader's reading of the week's notes, organised by term and never by who wrote what. Inputs this run: 61 notes across 14 source folders; 272 canonical terms in the vocabulary. Links point inside Tech Trends/; the analyst's inferences are marked as such.

Research Notes

A research note is the Trend Reader's deep read of one topic from the Research Queue. Each weekly brief ends with "Trends to analyse further"; those topics are appended to the queue as proposed. Every Monday run researches one topic automatically. It picks the proposed topic with the most evidence behind it, then the one reports raised most recently, then the oldest; the ranking is printed in the run report so the choice can be checked. The row is marked researching while the note is written and researched, with a link, once it is filed. To choose next week's topic yourself, set a row to researching in the queue file; done and dropped stay yours alone.

Every note follows the same shape so notes can be compared: the claim, stated precisely (what exactly is being asserted); evidence (figures and dated statements, each attributed to the person who made them); contradictions (where the sources disagree, with the Trend Reader's inference marked as such); what no source has connected or measured; and what would falsify the thesis. Notes are analysis to write from, never drafts in Andreas's voice; content angles go to the Writing Queue instead.

Research note · 2026-09-07

The system around the model as the unit of competition

Not "the model does not matter" — the corpus refutes that directly. The claim the evidence supports is narrower: the model decision has the shortest half-life of any decision in an AI stack, so the durable investment is the machinery that makes re-deciding cheap.

Question
Does the model decision have the shortest half-life in an AI stack, and what is durable instead?
Raised in
2026-W37 (analyst report, thesis 1)
Status
researched

What the note covers

  1. The claim, stated precisely
  2. Evidence
  3. Contradictions
  4. What no source has connected
  5. The durability ranking the evidence supports
  6. What would falsify this
  7. Sources read in full

Research note for the Research Queue row of the same name, raised in 2026-W37 Analyst Report as thesis 1. Seventeen post notes were read in full for this note; the digests were not used. Everything here is the Trend Reader's reading of published material, with positions attributed to the person who stated them.

The claim, stated precisely

Not "the model does not matter" — the corpus refutes that directly. The claim the evidence supports is narrower: the model decision has the shortest half-life of any decision in an AI stack, so the durable investment is the machinery that makes re-deciding cheap.

Evidence

One model, two harnesses, 37 points. Willison reports Astra scoring 99.9 percent on ARC-AGI-3 for $19,000 using OpenAI's custom Provider Adapter harness, which keeps reasoning state between requests and compacts long conversations, against 62.7 percent for $26,000 on the default harness. Better result, lower cost, identical weights. From GPT-6 Astra.

One model, five settings, 33x. The same prompt through Fable 5.1: low 1,998 output tokens at 10.0 cents, medium 1,977 at 9.9 cents, high 2,612 at 13.1 cents, then xhigh 36,767 at $1.83 and max 65,927 at $3.30. Three of the five settings are effectively the same model; the dial is a cliff rather than a curve. From Claude Fable 5.1 made me a really nice animated pelican.

The production case. Ramp's Inspect raises 75 percent of all merged pull requests (60 percent in January), passed one million sessions in July, is maintained by 5.5 people, hosts more than 200 internal agents, and is over 80 percent written by itself. The stated advantage is access to internal tools and data plus Self-verification through tests, telemetry, feature flags and screenshots, not the underlying model. Block (Goose), Stripe (Minions) and Shopify (River) run the same pattern. From Why Ramp built its own in-house coding agent, Inspect (paid post, 1,858 of 5,876 words read).

Model choice can be revoked by someone else. OpenAI ended Cursor's access, about 5 percent of Cursor's traffic, effective 12 November, after SpaceX acquired it; Anthropic had already limited Windsurf after Google's purchase, revoked OpenAI's Claude access, and cut off xAI in January 2026, yet raised Cursor's allocation because it rents SpaceX compute. Tunguz documents the downstream half: Salesforce making Claude the default across Agentforce and Slack, GLM-5.3's flagship requiring a security review for hosts above $10 billion of revenue, and Fable unavailable outside the US for three weeks under an export order before returning at a 1.1x surcharge. From Cursor just got BANNED (It's because of Elon...) (video) and The Price of Entry to the Frontier.

Accuracy does not survive contact with deployment. READY qualifies two agent systems 0.3 points apart in autonomous accuracy (72.8 versus 72.5 percent) and finds they need 39.2 versus 29.6 percent human review to reach the same 76 percent reliability target: a roughly 30 percent difference in operating cost hidden behind a rounding error in the score. From READY or Not: Reliable Enterprise Agent Deployment.

The clock. Frontier labs ship every 51 days on average in the agentic era, and open models closed the gap in 4.8 to 6 months, against 8.5 and 13 months in the two earlier eras. From Are Open Models Catching Up?. Osmani's practitioner version: engineering alpha is whatever models are currently bad at, and it decays with every release. From From Chrome DevTools to AI Engineering, with Addy Osmani.

Contradictions

1. Model choice matters enormously; it simply does not stay decided. Berman's Gemini 3.8 Flash review is the sharpest counter-evidence in the vault: Terminal-Bench 2.1 at 89.4 and first place, Terminal-Bench 4.0 at 19.1 against Opus 5's 51, first on Harvey's legal benchmark at 61.4, OSWorld 59 against 75. Cost per task spans $167 for Astra at max effort against $9 for GLM 5.3 Flash. His own advice is to build Internal benchmarks and choose per job. Trend Reader's inference: impact and durability are different axes. The model decision is high-impact and expires in weeks, which makes it recurring rather than unimportant; any published version of this thesis has to say so or it will read as "models do not matter", which the corpus refutes. From GOOGLE IS BACK! (Gemini 3.8 Flash) (video) and GPT-6 IS HERE!!! (ASTRA) (livestream).

2. Model-agnostic architecture is declining as a product and thriving as an architecture. Tunguz marks it declining, arguing the pluggable ideal survived mainly in coding tools while mainstream SaaS ships a hardcoded default; Berman forecasts that multi-provider platforms fade as labs verticalise into their own models, harnesses and coding products. Against that, six enterprises (Uber, Pinterest, Stripe, Coinbase, Ramp, AT&T) cut AI bills by roughly half through Smart model routing, and Ramp chose the open-source OpenCode harness specifically because it was model-agnostic. Trend Reader's inference: buying agnosticism from a vendor imports that vendor's supply risk, as Cursor and Windsurf show; building it internally does not. The term graph already carries the tension as two contrast pairs, Model-agnostic architecture against Smart model routing and against Access rationing. From The Pulse: tech companies move to open AI models.

3. A harness is durable only when it is yours. Cursor was acquired and then cut off; Windsurf was limited after its acquisition; Berman abandoned OpenClaw for Grok Bot inside a month over maintenance burden. A third-party harness is exposed to mergers and to lab retaliation, neither of which the buyer controls, while a model swap is a configuration change. Trend Reader's inference: on ownership volatility the ranking inverts, and a vendor harness is less durable than a model.

4. The labs are re-bundling model and harness. SemiAnalysis dates the agentic era from Opus 4.5 because of the "model-plus-harness product"; Anthropic ships Claude Code, OpenAI is merging Codex into ChatGPT, Google gates a Cyber variant through its Fair Wind programme. If bundling wins, choosing a model is choosing a harness and the separation this thesis depends on collapses. Trend Reader's inference: it collapses at the consumer and SaaS end, and holds where there is internal engineering to build the seam, which scopes the argument to enterprises.

5. The flagship number is a benchmark number. The 37-point swing is an evaluation harness on an abstract-reasoning benchmark, not a production workload. The vault's only production evidence for the harness thesis is Ramp's 75 percent, from a paid post read at 32 percent coverage. The one note dedicated to model-agnostic harness design, Berman's Nader Dabit interview, is a topic map only because the video was members-only and no transcript could be obtained (Nader Dabit: Devin, Open-source, Harnesses, Software Factories, and more!). The pro-agnostic case is the thinnest-sourced part of the argument.

What no source has connected

  • The configuration grid. Willison's 33x within one model and Berman's 18x across models compose: the same task spans roughly three orders of magnitude by configuration alone. Berman's own example proves the interaction, with Kimi K3 at 84 cents per task against GPT-5.6 Sol's 96 cents despite half the per-token price, because it consumes more tokens. No source prices the full grid of model by effort level.
  • Migration cost. "Models are easy to swap, harnesses are not" is asserted by three sources and measured by none. Nothing in 123 notes costs either migration.

The durability ranking the evidence supports

Longest half-life first, as the Trend Reader's inference from the above:

  1. Proprietary data and internal context (Harvey fine-tuning Kimi K3 into a legal specialist; over 151,000 Qwen derivatives; Ramp's tool and data access).
  2. Verification loops and the evaluation apparatus, including Internal benchmarks and Deployment qualification.
  3. Agent-legibility of one's own systems: documentation and exposed schemas as a procurement criterion, from Previously Unmanufacturable.
  4. A harness you own or can fork.
  5. Compute and access contracts, including model-switching rights, Zero Data Retention and Data sovereignty terms.
  6. The model.

What would falsify this

  • A vendor harness surviving an access cut-off without customer loss, which would weaken contradiction 3.
  • A measured migration cost showing model swaps are as expensive as harness swaps.
  • Bundled model-plus-harness products winning enterprise share against internally built agents over two or more quarters.
  • A production study, rather than a benchmark, showing harness choice accounts for less variance than model choice on the same workload.

Sources read in full

The seventeen notes linked above, plus The Most Important Chart In AI Right Now, GPT-6 Astra: an automated AI Engineer you can hire for <$6 an hour, Improving Evaluation Realism with Inference-Time Compute and Deployment Scaffolds and AgentX - InferenceXv3: Does CUDA Moat Hold up in Agentic Inferencing?. Coverage caveat: Berman supplies 39 percent of the corpus and both the strongest counter-evidence (spiky models, cost spread) and the verticalisation forecast; those two claims should be confirmed against Willison and Tunguz before publication, which the notes above allow.

Research note · 2026-09-13

Who finances open models, and what the long tail looks like

The evidence supports a split answer: open models are paid for by three groups whose returns come from something other than the model itself, and they finance different parts of the ecosystem. Nvidia funds the US open ecosystem and its distribution layer as demand creation for its chips, and separately underwrites the credit of the operators that buy those chips. Chinese frontier open labs, which Lambert says have…

Question
Lambert's Nvidia thesis and the on-prem long tail against Tunguz's debt math and Berman's Chinese-chip dependency; where does sovereign-model financing come from if not Nvidia? (Now also SemiAnalysis's Korea and open-model posts.)
Raised in
2026-W36 (report c)
Status
researched

What the note covers

  1. The claim, stated precisely
  2. Evidence
  3. Contradictions between sources
  4. What no source has connected or measured
  5. What the evidence supports
  6. What would falsify the thesis
  7. Sources read in full

Research note for the Research Queue row of the same name, raised in 2026-W36 Analyst Report c. Twenty-five post notes were read in full; the digests and analyst reports were not used as evidence. Everything here is the Trend Reader's reading of published material, with positions attributed to the person who stated them.

The claim, stated precisely

The evidence supports a split answer: open models are paid for by three groups whose returns come from something other than the model itself, and they finance different parts of the ecosystem. Nvidia funds the US open ecosystem and its distribution layer as demand creation for its chips, and separately underwrites the credit of the operators that buy those chips. Chinese frontier open labs, which Lambert says have led open models since about 2024, draw on state-directed capital markets, deployment revenue and revenue-threshold licences. States fund sovereign models, where the model costs tens of millions and the compute under it costs hundreds of billions, and in every sovereign case read that compute is Nvidia hardware. The enterprise long tail is paid for by buyers' own cost savings and, in the evidence, runs mostly on hosted inference providers rather than on premises.

Evidence

Nvidia's direct open-model spending is about $46 billion of named commitments. Tunguz lists $13 billion into Hugging Face, $7 billion into Poolside's model factory and a $26 billion five-year commitment to Nemotron, and reads open weights as Nvidia's defence against its own cloud customers and against sovereign restrictions. From The Price of Entry to the Frontier. Berman's daily reports Nvidia confirming a $12.9 billion purchase of Hugging Face (3 million models, over 18 million developers). From Forward Future Daily: China's AI Supply Chain, NVIDIA's Big Bets, & OpenAI Astra.

Lambert's thesis is dependence with a deadline. Nathan Lambert argues Nemotron's reported $26 billion releases data and training code so many companies build "token machines" that consume Nvidia chips, which makes the open ecosystem increasingly dependent on Nvidia's financing during a window in which the approach must pay back. He sets out two futures: open models keep pace, or they fork toward efficiency, modifiability and specialisation, filling a long tail of on-premises enterprise agents while closed models hold the high-value domains. He expects the second. From Teaching Everyone to Fish for Tokens.

Nvidia's credit exposure to its buyers is an order of magnitude larger than its open-model spending. SemiAnalysis's Compute, Capital and Markets team reads Nvidia's 10-Q: $530 billion of off-balance-sheet commitments against $184 billion a quarter earlier; about 6.5 GW of capacity backstopped; an AI Cloud Partner floor of about $2.35 per GB300 GPU-hour against $4.50 to $4.60 at market; new deals paused two weeks before publication. From Nvidia's Backstop Universe – Heads I Win, Tails Who Loses? (42 percent preview). Tunguz reports Neo clouds supplying the majority of Nvidia's net-new data centre revenue for the first time and days sales outstanding jumping from 45 to 60 in one quarter. From NVIDIA's $108b Quarter. Berman's dailies add a $500 billion GPU financing effort with Wall Street firms and The Economist's estimate of customer credit exposure reaching $200 billion by early 2029. From Forward Future Daily: NVIDIA AI Money Move, AI Taxes, & Google's Spirit Data Deal and the 4 September daily above.

The debt math sets the revenue bar the whole stack has to clear. Tunguz estimates about $4 trillion of new data centre debt over five years at 65 to 75 percent project leverage, requiring $1.2 to $1.5 trillion of annual AI revenue by 2030 from $100 to $200 billion today, a 55 percent compound growth rate. From Concrete, Silicon, & Leverage (AI infrastructure debt, AI revenue requirement).

Chinese open labs sit inside a separate capital pool and earn their own revenue. Berman's daily reports Chinese tech companies raising about $217 billion of equity over two years as Beijing directs its capital markets toward AI, plus $38 billion of bonds at more than 300 basis points below comparable US rates. From Forward Future Daily: China's AI Funding, Autonomous Hacks, & On-Device AI. Lambert reports Z.ai reportedly at $1 billion of annual recurring revenue driven by on-premises deployment. From GLM-5.3: How Chinese labs keep stride with the frontier. The Chinese-chip thread in the notes read concerns components more than training: China supplies about two-thirds of global optical transceivers for data centres (the 4 September daily); the one training run on Chinese accelerators recorded in these notes is Meituan's LongCat-2.0 (1.6 trillion parameters) on Ascend. From Latest open artifacts (#23): Laguna S2.1, Inkling, & Kimi K3 show the utility of open models on the Pareto frontier (Chinese AI chips).

Threshold licences are the one mechanism that charges for open weights directly, and among frontier-scale models only Chinese labs use them so far. Florian Brand describes Google and Meta moving to Apache 2.0 while Chinese frontier makers tighten: Kimi K3 requires commercial agreements from providers, MiniMax M3 sets a revenue threshold, and GLM 5.3 moved from MIT to a licence requiring a Z.AI security review above $10 billion of revenue with affiliates, a term left undefined in the English text. From Latest open artifacts (#24): Motif-3, GLM-5.3, Hy4-preview and open model licenses (partial). David Krevitt reports the Kimi K3 threshold at $20 million of 12-month revenue and the same $3 input and $15 output price per million tokens at every provider; Liquid AI's LFM2.5-2.6B is free for commercial use below $10 million. From Dispatch: Kimi K3 licensing, Liquid AI, and stacked PRs on GitHub. Tunguz's phrase for the pattern is "open until you scale" (Revenue-share licenses).

A sovereign model costs tens of millions; the sovereign compute plan costs hundreds of billions. SemiAnalysis (Patel, Kan, Wang, Xie) reports Korea's elimination-round tournament at a government budget of about $350 million, with Motif 3 trained from scratch for about $15 million on 768 B200s by a company with $17 million raised, scoring above Inkling and Nemotron 3 Ultra, then eliminated on expert review and non-blind user testing. The accompanying data centre plan is $919 billion for 8.4 GW by 2029 and 18.4 GW by 2035, led by SK Group, GS Group and Naver. The authors argue the only complete hedge against losing frontier API access is to pretrain on one's own GPUs. From Korea's Trillion-Dollar Sovereign AI Investment: Nvidia Wins, Hynix Loses (75 percent preview; paid conclusion unread). A.X K2, a 688-billion-parameter Korean model trained on about 8.5 trillion tokens, serves in 4-bit NVFP4 within one point of FP8. From A.X K2 Technical Report (abstract only). Trend Reader inference: NVFP4 is Nvidia's 4-bit format, so the serving result is tied to Nvidia hardware. Jack Clark reports Five Eyes ministers seeking timely government access to frontier models. From Import AI 471: Why Hugging Face worries me; space mining; Five Eyes on AI (Frontier model access).

The long tail is visible, and it is mostly hosted. Orosz reports Uber cutting cost per AI request by 34 percent and per session by 52 percent with open models on inference services at 2 to 20 times lower cost; Pinterest's open models post-trained on its own data at under 8 percent of closed-model cost per transaction; AT&T cutting some advanced-task costs by up to 56 percent with a 2 percent quality drop through the LiteLLM router; and Ramp data showing AI spend at the top 1 percent of businesses down 10 percent in August. From The Pulse: tech companies move to open AI models (blog republish) (Smart model routing). Berman's daily, citing The New York Times, has AT&T at up to 80 percent savings with open models at 40 percent of its AI use (20 percent in May) and US open-model usage share rising from 10 to 58 percent in a year. From Forward Future Daily: AI Jobs Boom, Rogue Agents, & the Rise of Open Models. Brand reports Thinking Machines' fine-tuning service at reportedly hundreds of millions a year and that predicted Lab consolidation has not arrived (the Latest open artifacts 23 note). Will Raphaelson reports 70 percent of derivative models built on Qwen. From What's going on with open-weight models? (64 percent preview).

The capability gap keeps closing, which is Lambert's first future. SemiAnalysis measures the Open-closed capability gap closing in about 13 months, then 8.5, then 4.8 to 6 months across three eras. From Are Open Models Catching Up? (76 percent preview). Lambert puts it at 4 to 6 months. From Open-Source AI & Open Models Reading List. Tunguz reports the best open-weight model at 80 percent of frontier score by May, from 48 percent a year earlier. From Honestly, Who Buys SOTA?. Berman prices DeepSeek V4.1 Flash at $0.15 per million input tokens off-peak and says cheaper models suffice for 95 percent of use cases. From Deepseek did it again... (video).

Distillation is an unpriced input to Chinese open models. Anthropic's threat report names seven Chinese labs, including an Alibaba campaign of over 151 million exchanges from May to July used to train Qwen 3.5 to 3.7. From Detecting and countering misuse of AI: September 2026 (one party's forensic claims). John Schulman calls distillation the main force against centralisation. From AI researchers debate how close we are to recursive self-improvement (Distillation attacks).

Contradictions between sources

1. Lambert's dependence thesis describes the US open ecosystem only. Lambert says open models increasingly depend on Nvidia; his own reading list says the leading open models have come from Chinese labs since about 2024, and none of the Chinese financing evidence (the $217 billion of equity, Z.ai's $1 billion on-premises revenue, threshold licences) runs through Nvidia's open-model programme. Trend Reader inference: the thesis holds for Nemotron, Poolside, Hugging Face and the US models Motif 3 outscored, and the question "who finances open models" has two separate answers by geography.

2. The long tail Lambert predicts and the long tail sources observe differ in location. Lambert names on-premises enterprise agents. Every Western enterprise case with figures (Uber, Pinterest, AT&T) runs on inference providers or routers; the only on-premises revenue figure in the notes read is Z.ai's, in China. Trend Reader inference: specialisation and post-training are observed as predicted, while on-premises deployment in Western enterprises is asserted and unmeasured.

3. "Open until you scale" is a Chinese trend in Brand's account. Tunguz reads open source as narrowing overall; Brand records Google and Meta moving to Apache 2.0 over the same period, and Berman's 11 August daily lists Meta's Muse Glimmer under Apache 2.0. The thresholds themselves span $10 million (Liquid AI), $20 million (Kimi K3) and $10 billion (GLM-5.3), three orders of magnitude, so the phrase covers mechanisms aimed at small inference hosts and at the largest groups alike.

4. The open shift Nvidia funds reduces the revenue per token that services the debt Nvidia backstops. Tunguz's debt math requires AI revenue to grow 55 percent a year; Orosz's cases show spend per request falling 34 percent at Uber and top-firm AI spend down 10 percent in August. Uber's usage rose with cost flat, so volume can offset price. Trend Reader inference: no source models whether open-model price elasticity raises or lowers total revenue against that requirement, which decides whether Nvidia's two financing roles pull in the same direction.

5. The figures on Nvidia's balance sheet do not reconcile. SemiAnalysis reports cash of $22 billion, up from $12 billion, and calls the risk asymmetric in Nvidia's favour; The Economist, via Berman, cites a $99 billion cash pile as the reason this differs from dot-com vendor financing; Tunguz treats the DSO jump as the item to watch. Trend Reader inference: the cash figures probably measure different things, and the difference matters for the soundness case.

6. Hugging Face is described as an investment and as a purchase. Tunguz lists $13 billion into Hugging Face as spending to keep ecosystems open; Berman's daily five days later reports a confirmed $12.9 billion purchase. Gaurav Sharma, io.net's CEO in a guest essay for Berman, reports parts of Nvidia's July revenue-sharing programme paused over antitrust concerns. From The GPU Access Problem NVIDIA Can't Solve Alone. Trend Reader inference: owning the main distribution hub for open weights is also control over it.

7. The two AT&T accounts use different measures. Orosz (via The Information) reports up to 56 percent lower cost on some tasks with a 2 percent quality drop; Berman's daily (via The New York Times) reports up to 80 percent savings. Neither note states the base.

8. The weight of distillation is disputed. Lambert says it helps Chinese labs without explaining their progress; Schulman calls it the main force against centralisation; Anthropic supplies volumes. Trend Reader inference: to the extent distillation lowers Chinese open labs' costs, part of the open frontier is financed by US closed labs' training spend, and Anthropic's countermeasures would move that cost back onto the Chinese labs.

What no source has connected or measured

  • Nvidia's open-model spending as a share of anything. Trend Reader arithmetic: $26 billion over five years is about $5.2 billion a year against a $432 billion annualised revenue run rate, about 1.2 percent; the three named commitments together (about $46 billion) are less than half of one quarter's $96 billion revenue. No source sets them against the $530 billion of commitments or asks what payback would look like.
  • Revenue from threshold licences. No note reports what Moonshot, MiniMax or Zhipu collect, or whether any host above a threshold has signed.
  • Whether the inference providers serving open models are backstopped. Krevitt names Modal, Baseten, Fireworks and Together AI among others; the AI Cloud Partner deals named are Firmus, SharonAI and GMI. The link between the enterprise long tail and Nvidia's credit is undocumented.
  • How a sovereign plan divides its financing. Korea's model budget (about $350 million) is roughly 1/2,600 of its $919 billion data centre plan. Trend Reader arithmetic, for illustration only: at Tunguz's 65 to 75 percent leverage the plan would carry about $600 to $690 billion of debt; the Korea post does not give the structure, and Nvidia reports sovereign revenue only inside a combined category.
  • A sovereign model on non-Nvidia compute. No note in this set shows a state financing a model on TPUs or other Custom silicon.
  • The Gulf. None of the 25 notes contains evidence on Gulf sovereign financing; the GCC lines in their relevance sections are the Trend Reader's own.
  • The cost saving from distillation, which nobody has estimated.

What the evidence supports

  1. "Nvidia finances open models" is accurate for the US open ecosystem and its distribution layer, at about $46 billion of named commitments, and inaccurate for the Chinese labs that lead open-model capability.
  2. Nvidia's larger financing role is credit to compute buyers ($530 billion of commitments, 6.5 GW backstopped), which supports open models only indirectly and where two pauses have been reported (new AI Cloud Partner deals; parts of the July revenue-sharing programme).
  3. Chinese open labs combine state-directed capital, deployment revenue and threshold licences, and possibly a distillation subsidy of unmeasured size.
  4. For a state, the model is the cheap part: Korea shows a near-frontier model for about $15 million inside a $919 billion compute plan. The answer to "if not Nvidia" for sovereign models, in this evidence, is state budgets for the model and domestic conglomerates for the compute, running on Nvidia hardware.
  5. The enterprise long tail is funded by the buyer's savings (34 to 92 percent in the cases reported), is specialised and post-trained as Lambert predicted, and is hosted rather than on premises in every Western case with figures.

What would falsify the thesis

  • A Western lab adopting a revenue-threshold licence for a flagship open model, or a Chinese lab returning its flagship to MIT, which would undo the geographic split in point 3.
  • Nvidia cutting Nemotron or restricting Hugging Face after the purchase while US open releases continue at the same pace, which would show the US ecosystem is less dependent than Lambert argues.
  • A state training and serving a sovereign model on non-Nvidia compute at scale.
  • Nvidia's DSO returning to the 43-to-46-day band and the AI Cloud Partner programme resuming, which would weaken the fragility reading of point 2.
  • Measured on-premises open-model deployment in Western enterprises at a scale comparable to the hosted cases.
  • Open-model usage share falling as frontier prices fall, which would show the long tail is priced by closed-model prices and does not stand on its own economics.

Sources read in full

  • Latest open artifacts (#24): Motif-3, GLM-5.3, Hy4-preview and open model licenses

Not read under the 25-note cap and worth checking before publication: TPU Inference Externalization Full Steam Ahead - InferenceX for a non-Nvidia compute route, and The Most Important Chart In AI Right Now for token-share data.

Research note · 2026-09-14

The two-market thesis with numbers

The sources describe two markets priced on different units: a volume market in which open and near-frontier models carry most tokens at a small share of spend, and a premium market in which closed frontier models carry a minority of tokens and most of the revenue. The four figures in the question measure different things and, combined, support a narrower claim than "the frontier premium is disappearing": the premium…

Question
How long does a frontier premium last and who pays for the open side? Combine the 23x token-versus-revenue chart, 84 percent non-SOTA tokens, the five-month gap per era and the 51-day cadence.
Raised in
2026-W37
Status
researched

What the note covers

  1. The claim, stated precisely
  2. Evidence
  3. Contradictions between sources
  4. What no source has connected or measured
  5. What the evidence supports
  6. What would falsify the thesis
  7. Sources read in full

Research note for the Research Queue row of the same name, first raised in 2026-W37 Analyst Report and last raised in 2026-W37 Analyst Report c. Twenty-two post notes were read in full; the digests and analyst reports were not used as evidence. Everything here is the Trend Reader's reading of published material, with positions attributed to the person who stated them. Financing of open models is covered in 2026-09-13 Who finances open models, and what the long tail looks like and is referenced here, not repeated.

The claim, stated precisely

The sources describe two markets priced on different units: a volume market in which open and near-frontier models carry most tokens at a small share of spend, and a premium market in which closed frontier models carry a minority of tokens and most of the revenue. The four figures in the question measure different things and, combined, support a narrower claim than "the frontier premium is disappearing": the premium attached to any one frontier model lasts about one closed release interval, while the premium attached to the frontier labs persists, because each lab replaces its model roughly three times before open models match the first one. The open side is paid for mainly by buyers converting frontier spend into savings through hosts and routers, which shows up as token volume rather than as revenue anyone reports.

Evidence

The token-versus-revenue gap is about 23x on spend share and about 40x on list price. Berman reads a Vercel chart for June to August: DeepSeek 25.2 percent of tokens and 2.8 percent of spend, Anthropic 24.5 percent of tokens and 64.6 percent of spend. He relays Gavin Baker's framing that top models take about 50 percent of tokens and about 90 percent of revenue, and gives run rates of more than $65 billion for Anthropic and about $40 billion for OpenAI, more than every open-model provider combined. From The Most Important Chart In AI Right Now (Token share versus revenue share). Trend Reader arithmetic: per unit of token share, Anthropic earns 2.64 units of spend share and DeepSeek 0.11, a ratio of about 24. Tunguz's OpenRouter figures: 84 percent of tokens go to models that are not state of the art; the six models carrying 80 percent of volume deliver about 77 percent of frontier performance at a blended $0.50 per million tokens against about $20 for Fable 5. From Honestly, Who Buys SOTA?.

A new frontier model captures a small share of its own lab's usage. Tunguz, from Ramp data: a month after launch Fable 5 had 6 percent of Anthropic tokens and 11 percent of Anthropic spend, and earned about 75 percent of GPT-5.6 Sol's model-attributed revenue in July despite a higher price; Sol held about a quarter of OpenAI's tokens. His extrapolation is that each release wins less share than the last, with the premium justified mainly for software architecture and security design (the SOTA post above). Berman says cheaper models suffice for 95 percent of use cases. From Deepseek did it again... (video).

The premium moves between closed labs at each release. Zvi's revealed-preference poll after GPT-6 Astra shows about a 15 to 16 percent net shift of primary use from Anthropic to OpenAI, which he expects to hold until the next release; he recommends running Astra and Fable 5.1 together. From GPT-6-Astra Can Do Ambitious Things.

Closed frontier cadence is 51 days; open catch-up is 4.8 to 6 months. SemiAnalysis measures the Open-closed capability gap across three eras on era-specific benchmarks: 35.8 points closed in about 13 months (early scaling), 12.1 points in 8.5 months (reasoning), and 4.8 months (Kimi K2.6 past Opus 4.5) and 6 months (GLM-5.2 past GPT-5.2) in the agentic era. In that era OpenAI and Anthropic shipped a model every 51 days on average, against 213 and 120 days before. The paid section, on why this is less bearish for frontier labs, was not read (76 percent preview). From Are Open Models Catching Up? (Release cadence). Lambert puts the gap at 4 to 6 months. From Open-Source AI & Open Models Reading List. Berman: about six months behind the previous frontier generation, and on local machines six months after that (the DeepSeek note above). Tunguz: the best open model at 80 percent of frontier score by May, from 48 percent a year earlier. Raphaelson: Artificial Analysis index 22 against 35 a year ago, now 54 to 57 against 50 to 61. From What's going on with open-weight models? (64 percent preview).

Trend Reader arithmetic on the two durations. 4.8 to 6 months is about 146 to 183 days, or 2.9 to 3.6 closed release intervals of 51 days. On SemiAnalysis's method, by the time an open model matches a closed model, that model's lab has shipped roughly three successors. Google's own release text shows the same pace at the Flash tier: Gemini 3.8 Flash is the third Flash release in six weeks, three weeks after 3.7 Flash. From Introducing Gemini 3.8 Flash and 3.8 Flash Cyber (the lab's claims).

Chinese open labs compress the other side of the race. Lambert attributes Z.ai's position to cadence: Chinese labs release within days of training while US labs test for months, so they climb during the US pre-release window; GLM-5.3 is about 750 billion parameters, the GLM-5.2 base with extended post-training, and Z.ai is reportedly at $1 billion ARR on on-premises deployments. From GLM-5.3: How Chinese labs keep stride with the frontier.

Per-task cost narrows or widens the per-token gap depending on token use. Berman: Kimi K3 at half GPT-5.6 Sol's per-token price costs 84 cents per task against Sol's 96 cents (the chart note); GLM 5.3 Flash costs 9 cents per Artificial Analysis task against $3.14 for Fable 5 and 95 cents for Sol, while using about 47,000 output tokens per task against about 20,000 for GPT-5.6 Luna Max. From Cancel your subscriptions, Ox-Alpha is here! (GLM 5.3 Flash). Fable 5.1 keeps Fable 5's list prices, cuts cache reads 75 percent to $0.25, and uses 1.7 times Fable 5's output tokens. From Anthropic went CRAZY (Mythos/Fable 5.1) (video).

Frontier prices are moving in both directions. Price cuts: GPT-5.6 Luna by 80 percent and Sol by 20 to 33 percent (Berman's chart note). Holds or rises: Astra at $10 input and $50 output per million, about 60 percent more expensive than Sol in practice per Artificial Analysis (Zvi); Orosz reports Anthropic getting more expensive while open and OpenAI models get cheaper, with an Opus 5 agent run at 100 times the cost of GPT-5.6 Luna xhigh or DeepSeek. From The Pulse: tech companies move to open AI models (blog republish) (Frontier model pricing). Google prices Gemini 3.8 Flash at $0.75 and $3.75 as an introductory rate that doubles on 1 January 2027. At the bottom, DeepSeek V4.1 Flash is $0.15 input and $0.60 output off-peak (Berman).

The open side is paid for by buyers' savings, routed through hosts. Orosz: Uber's cost per request down 34 percent and per session 52 percent, total cost flat since March while usage rose, open models 2 to 20 times cheaper; Pinterest under 8 percent of closed-model cost per transaction; AT&T up to 56 percent lower cost on some tasks for a 2 percent quality drop; a Databricks survey ranks open models first and routing second among cost levers; Ramp reports top-1-percent firms' AI spend down 10 percent in August (the republish above; the original issue was a preview, The Pulse: tech companies move to open AI models). Berman's daily, citing The New York Times: AT&T at up to 80 percent savings with open models at 40 percent of use (20 percent in May), and US open-model usage share from 10 to 58 percent in a year. From Forward Future Daily: AI Jobs Boom, Rogue Agents, & the Rise of Open Models. On the host side: Fireworks serves over 40 trillion tokens a day, twice the OpenAI API's March volume (SemiAnalysis); GLM 5.3 Flash is reported at over 100 trillion tokens a day on Chinese chips only (SemiAnalysis via Berman's GLM note). Berman names enterprise reasons beyond price: ownership, bargaining power across Neo clouds, fine-tuning on proprietary data, and 151,000-plus Qwen derivatives (the chart note).

Volume growth is agent-driven, and agents route to cheap models. Tunguz: OpenRouter agent volume up 14x in six months (0.51 to 7.3 trillion tokens) against 2.8x for humans, with the caveat that OpenRouter's mix skews toward open-weight models and most agent tokens are cached prompts. From The Three Waves of AI Consumption. Uber sends Sub-agents to cheaper models (Orosz). OpenAI's median researcher went from $14 to over $600 a day of inference in under five months. From Is the 3x AI Productivity Gain just a Computer that Never Sleeps?.

Two sources name a third segment in between. Christian Catalini, in an Original for Forward Future, predicts commodity tokens metered like electricity and value-added tokens that combine open models with an enterprise's own continually generated data. From No, Anthropic Won't Be the Only Company Left. Berman relays Catalini's three-way split (commodity open, state-of-the-art open specialists, closed frontier generalists), with Harvey's Kimi K3 legal model as the middle case. Lambert expects open models to fork toward a specialised long tail while closed models hold the most valuable domains. From Teaching Everyone to Fish for Tokens.

Access gating creates premium tiers that are not priced per token. Tunguz lists Mythos 5 through Project Glasswing, Fable returning outside the US as US-only inference at a 1.1x surcharge, OpenAI's government variants to trusted partners, Salesforce hardcoding Claude, and OpenAI cutting off Cursor. From The Price of Entry to the Frontier (Access rationing). Gemini 3.8 Flash Cyber is available only through the Fairwind programme (the Frontier Labs note).

Contradictions between sources

1. The share figures come from gateways, and the gateways disagree with the run rates. Vercel's chart, OpenRouter's usage and The New York Times' US figure are the only market-wide share data in the notes read; none is first-party lab data, and Tunguz states OpenRouter skews to open models. Trend Reader arithmetic: if Vercel's 64.6 percent Anthropic spend share were market-wide, OpenAI's roughly $40 billion against Anthropic's $65 billion would need about 40 percent of spend, leaving almost nothing for Google, xAI and every open host. Trend Reader inference: Vercel's customer mix leans to Anthropic and coding, so the 23x describes that gateway, not the market.

2. A shrinking premium per release and growing frontier revenue. Tunguz says each release wins less share; Berman and SemiAnalysis report Anthropic past $65 billion run rate, and SemiAnalysis says GLM 5.3 and Kimi K3 now do the coding and agentic work that got it there. Trend Reader inference: both hold if the premium market grows in absolute size while each model's share of it falls; no source separates the two effects.

3. Three "cadence" figures measure different things. SemiAnalysis's 51 days is the average gap between OpenAI and Anthropic frontier releases; Tunguz's "a new model every three days" counts state-of-the-art releases across all labs; Lambert's "days versus months" is the lag from finished training to release. Only the first bears on how long a closed model is the frontier.

4. Catch-up is benchmark time; practitioners dispute the benchmarks. SemiAnalysis's months are on era-normalised benchmarks run mostly on Prime Intellect's stack. Berman finds DeepSeek V4.1 Flash benchmarking level with Opus 5 and Sol but failing his own tests, and is unsure it beats GLM 5.3; report c's thesis 5 records benchmarks losing authority. Tunguz's 80 percent is a score ratio, not a lag, and Raphaelson's index figures put the best open models above GPT-5.5 and Claude Opus.

5. Per-token gaps and per-task gaps diverge. Blended OpenRouter prices differ by about 40x per token; Kimi K3 against Sol differs by 1.14x per task; GLM 5.3 Flash against Fable 5 by about 35x per task. Berman's Fable 5.1 figures mix units ($369 for an Artificial Analysis run against $1.23, $0.68 and $0.43 for other models), which the note does not reconcile. Trend Reader inference: token hunger (47,000 against 20,000 output tokens per task; Fable 5.1 at 1.7 times Fable 5) moves the premium by more than list-price changes do.

6. Is the scarce thing capability or access? Tunguz's SOTA post argues price elasticity erodes the premium; his Price of Entry post two weeks later argues access, not price, is becoming the scarcity at the frontier. Trend Reader inference: the two describe different buyers, application builders who route on price and enterprises or states buying the gated tier, and they imply different lifetimes for the premium.

7. Who funds the open side depends on the source's vantage. Orosz and Berman's daily show buyers' savings; Lambert and Tunguz show Nvidia's financing; SemiAnalysis shows hosts at tens of trillions of tokens a day; Anthropic's threat report, covered in the 2026-09-13 note, adds distillation as an unpriced transfer from closed labs. None of these sources sums them.

What no source has connected or measured

  • The half-life of one model's premium. The only post-launch share figure is Fable 5 at one month (6 percent of tokens, 11 percent of spend). No source tracks a frontier model's spend share at two, four and six months, which is the direct measure of the question.
  • Revenue on the open side. Fireworks' 40 trillion tokens a day and GLM 5.3 Flash's 100 trillion carry no revenue figures; Z.ai's $1 billion ARR is the one open-lab revenue number. Trend Reader arithmetic, for scale only: 40 trillion tokens a day at OpenRouter's blended $0.50 per million would be about $20 million a day, about $7.3 billion a year; the notes give no host's actual price mix.
  • First-party token splits. No lab reports tokens by model tier; all share data is from gateways, routers or single companies.
  • Whether closed price cuts track open catch-up. Luna's 80 percent and Sol's 20 to 33 percent cuts are not dated against open releases in any note.
  • Where the value-added middle segment is priced. Catalini, Berman and Lambert name it; Tunguz's harness ARR multiples of 25x to 125x (AI Harness' ARR Multiples) price application companies built on both markets, but no source connects those multiples to which model tier the companies run on.
  • The share of frontier spend in the use cases Tunguz calls frontier-justified (architecture and security), against Berman's 95 percent that does not need the frontier.

What the evidence supports

  1. Two markets exist in gateway data, with frontier closed models earning roughly 20 to 40 times more per token than the cheapest open models; the size of the gap in the whole market is unmeasured.
  2. A given closed frontier model is the frontier for about 51 days before its own lab replaces it, and open models match it on benchmarks in 4.8 to 6 months; the model-level premium is therefore set mainly by closed-lab competition, and the lab-level premium by staying on the cadence.
  3. The premium is shared between closed labs at each release (Zvi's 15 to 16 percent shift) and eroded within a lab by its own cheaper tiers (Fable 5 at 6 percent of Anthropic tokens; Luna and Sol price cuts).
  4. The open side is paid for by buyers' savings (34 to 92 percent in the reported cases), delivered through hosts and routers, and grows with agent parallelism routed to cheap models; its revenue is not reported.
  5. Per-task cost, not list price, sets the practical gap, and token-hungry open models can close most of it (Kimi K3 against Sol) or none of it (GLM 5.3 Flash against Fable 5).

What would falsify the thesis

  • A frontier model holding or growing its share of its lab's tokens or spend two or more months after launch, which would contradict Tunguz's shrinking capture and point 2.
  • Open catch-up lengthening in the next era on SemiAnalysis's method (more than 6 months to match GPT-6 Astra or Fable 5.1).
  • The closed cadence slowing well beyond 51 days (for example after the post-incident safety testing Berman and Zvi describe) with no loss of frontier spend share, which would show the premium does not depend on cadence.
  • Open-model spend share rising toward token share (DeepSeek above 10 percent of Vercel spend, say), which would mean the volume market has started to monetise.
  • First-party lab data showing gateway figures materially overstate open-model token share.
  • Frontier lab run rates falling while open usage rises, which would show the two markets are substitutes rather than separate.

Sources read in full

  • No, Anthropic Won't Be the Only Company Left
  • Cancel your subscriptions, Ox-Alpha is here! (GLM 5.3 Flash)

Coverage caveat: the gap and cadence figures come from a 76 percent SemiAnalysis preview whose unread paid section argues the trend is less bearish for frontier labs; the Vercel and OpenRouter shares are relayed by Berman and Tunguz from gateway data; the Orosz 3 September issue was a preview and the figures used are from its free republish; the Frontier Labs note is Google's own account. The Artifacts Hub note was read but adds no figure on the two markets. Not read under the scope chosen: arXiv papers (none on pricing in W36) and the W37 Stratechery overviews.

Research note · 2026-09-16

Verification sized to the builder

The sources describe verification at three scales, and the evidence supports a single structure across them: what gets checked is decided by blast radius, and who can check it is decided by whether the check can be written down as a rule or has to be bought as a system. For a team of one, READY's profile collapses to a different question from the enterprise one: the oversight term is the builder's own hours, and the…

Question
What does READY's reliability-and-oversight-cost profile look like for a team of one? Take the AI-written-code thesis across its three scales (Meta and Ramp; closed contributions and stacked PRs; vibe coders and the 170-of-1,645 RLS scan) and separate the checks a non-engineer can run from the ones that must be bought.
Raised in
2026-W37 (report b)
Status
researched

What the note covers

  1. The claim, stated precisely
  2. Evidence
  3. Contradictions between sources
  4. What no source has connected or measured
  5. What the evidence supports
  6. What would falsify the thesis
  7. Sources read in full

Research note for the Research Queue row of the same name, first raised in 2026-W37 Analyst Report b and last raised in 2026-W38 Analyst Report b. Eighteen post notes were read in full; the digests and analyst reports were not used as evidence. Everything here is the Trend Reader's reading of published material, with positions attributed to the people who stated them.

The claim, stated precisely

The sources describe verification at three scales, and the evidence supports a single structure across them: what gets checked is decided by blast radius, and who can check it is decided by whether the check can be written down as a rule or has to be bought as a system. For a team of one, READY's profile collapses to a different question from the enterprise one: the oversight term is the builder's own hours, and the cheapest policy that meets the reliability target is the one that moves checks from review (reading the output) to constraint (making the failure impossible). Three checks in the evidence are writable by a non-engineer and cost only time: a written authorization plan, a database schema treated as fixed, and a blast-radius rule for what never gets shipped without a second pair of eyes. Everything else the sources describe as working at scale, independent test generation, guard models over execution traces, trace-level trust scoring and risk-classified review pipelines, is a trained or purchased system, and none of the sources prices it for an individual.

Evidence

READY defines the profile the topic asks about, at enterprise scale. The authors separate whether an agent can do the work from whether it meets a reliability target under acceptable oversight at tolerable cost, and qualify the cheapest oversight policy that reaches the target on held-out cases. Their clinical-audit study over 16 agent systems and 750 cases has two systems 0.3 points apart in autonomous accuracy (72.8 against 72.5 percent) needing 39.2 against 29.6 percent human review to reach the same 76 percent reliability. From READY or Not: Reliable Enterprise Agent Deployment (Deployment qualification). The unit is a share of cases reviewed by a person, which presumes people to review them.

At company scale, review volume is the constraint and triage is the answer. Orosz reports pull requests opened up fivefold over three years, with PRs and commits nearly doubling since the end of 2025, and three responses in use: humans reviewing the AI's review (seven vendors named; Anthropic's Bun runs three at once; Weaviate leaves about 90 percent to agents), triage by blast radius (confirmed at Anthropic and OpenAI; Duckbill labels public API, auth, design system, non-additive schema changes and agent skills as human-only), and reviewing the plan, tests or schema instead of the implementation. Duckbill's numbers: merged PRs 353 to 684, median merge 26 hours with human review against 1 hour without, with an 85 percent test-coverage floor as the price of the shift. From What is happening with code reviews? (Code review, Blast radius).

At lab scale the pipeline is rebuilt rather than staffed. At OpenAI, per Orosz's interviews, Codex gathers context, implements, babysits the PR through CI and a performance harness; multiple domain-specialist review agents check the change; changes are risk-classified with some areas opting into agent auto-approval of low-risk PRs; an agent shepherds rollout and builds its own dashboard; Perf Factory turns production alerts into proposed fixes and Sevbot proposes incident mitigations without executing them. Humans define outcomes, approve production changes and stay on call. Some build, test and deploy systems carry roughly 10x load in about six months. From Inside OpenAI’s agentic software factory (Software factory, AI incident response).

Open-source projects solved the same problem by refusing the input. Vercel's AI SDK factory uses separate agents to reproduce, fix and review bugs and, four weeks in, authors 25 to 35 percent of merged PRs and closes 70 to 80 percent of issues; Lars Grammel's stated reason is trust in a specific agent configuration with a track record over an unknown community agent. Astro converts external PRs into issues, tldraw closes them by policy, and Mitchell Hashimoto predicts large projects will close contributions entirely. From PRs NOT Welcome: How Top AI Open Source Projects Are Managing Thousands of Contributors.

The individual scale has one measured failure rate and three writable checks. A 2025 scan of 1,645 vibe-coded apps found 170 with missing or unenforced row-level security, exposing emails, API keys and payment data. Krevitt's remedies are written before the agent builds: an authorization plan as a table of roles against features, the rule never to trust the frontend, and enforcement at three points (page route, API middleware, database row-level security). From Software Eng for Vibe Coders: Auth + Security. The schema is the second: he calls it the written rulebook the code depends on, with the blast radius of a wrong type set by how defensively the code was written. From Software Eng for Vibe Coders: Databases + Storage. The third is the decision not to ship: Railway's growth lead ships internal tools built in under four hours and hands off anything touching traffic and scale, security (never the Stripe integration), defensive behaviour or monitoring, on two tests, what happens when it breaks and who is paged at 2am. From When not to vibe code (Vibe coding).

Where a non-engineering team did scale, it changed the environment rather than the review. Sentry's growth lead moved about 2,500 pages out of a CMS into Markdown in Git so agents could reach all of the site: 2.5 developers over two months, about 200 bespoke pages collapsed into three templates so the agent learns three patterns, broken staging builds down about 95 percent, build time 14 minutes to under 4. Output afterwards: 147 pages edited in 2 PRs, 116 internal-link PRs, 11 concurrent A/B tests. From How my non-engineering team at Sentry learned to ship. Trend Reader inference: the checks that made this safe are structural (templates, staging builds, Git history), not review of the agent's output.

Independent tests are the one verification the research prices, and self-written tests are worse than none. ExecCritic separates a Test agent from a Repair agent with a fail-closed harness that freezes qualified tests: on SWE-bench Verified, tests from the base Test agent cut the resolved rate from 61.2 to 57.3 percent, tests from GPT-5.6 Sol raised it to 65.3 percent, and two separately post-trained agents reached 72.6 percent, an 11.4-point gain. From ExecCritic: Learn to Test, Test to Improve for Coding Agents (Verification loops).

The checks that must be bought are trained systems. HazardAuditor runs Claude Code, Codex, Hermes and OpenClaw in controlled environments, normalises their interactions into canonical events and trains a guard with sequence-level optimisation, for up to 16.5 points over the strongest prior guard. From HazardAuditor: From Executable Threats to Safer Computer-Use Agents. AgentAudit scores whole execution traces on ten dimensions and finds composite trust from 95.1 (Claude Sonnet 5) to 22.6 (Gemini 2.5 Flash) among agents with comparable completion, with a single evaluated model as judge. From AgentAudit: An Open, Extensible Framework for Full-Lifecycle Trust Evaluation of AI Agents.

Reviewing the plan, the cheapest option for a solo builder, is the one with a demonstrated attack. Plan injection plants harmful but benign-sounding reasoning in the actor's context; monitors miss it 25 to 33 percent of the time, and giving the monitor the injected plan drops detection by up to 50 percent on one task. From Corrupt Plans, Clean Traces: Evading Chain-of-Thought Monitoring with Plan Injection (Chain-of-thought monitoring). The agent-security survey's framing is the general case: natural language is input, control code and protocol at once, which the author calls a Turing-complete blast radius. From Trustworthy Agentic AI: A Comprehensive Cybersecurity and Systems Survey on Threat Landscapes, Defense Architectures, and Open Challenges.

The cost of supervision is the term the practitioners emphasise. Nick Wentz defines an agent's value as the work avoided minus the cost of managing the delegation: choosing what to delegate, explaining it, connecting tools, granting permissions, monitoring, evaluating and fixing errors; his examples of drift include a lending agent skipping income verification 20 to 30 percent of the time while producing plausible recommendations. From Why Don't People Care About AI Agents Yet?. Tunguz's reading of OpenAI's own disclosure is the same term measured in shifts: 3.1 agent-workdays per human shift, more than half of successful four-to-eight-hour tasks still needing human intervention, and about 2x delivered work for 3x logged runtime. From Is the 3x AI Productivity Gain just a Computer that Never Sleeps?.

Contradictions between sources

1. READY's unit does not exist for a team of one. Its oversight policies are shares of cases reviewed by people; at 29.6 percent review on 750 cases that is a staffing decision. For a solo builder the same term is their own hours, and no source in this set measures oversight in hours per task. Trend Reader inference: the profile transfers, the policy class does not, and the individual version of "cheapest policy meeting the target" is mostly a choice of constraint (schema, permissions, blast radius) rather than of review rate.

2. Less human review is paid for with stricter automation, which the individual scale cannot assume. Duckbill's shift to risk labels came with an 85 percent test-coverage floor and stricter linting; OpenAI's auto-approval runs behind CI, a performance harness and specialist review agents. The vibe-coder material describes no test suite at all. Trend Reader inference: the enterprise trade is review effort for test infrastructure, and a builder without the second half is not choosing the same policy.

3. Agent-written tests are the obvious individual substitute and the one ExecCritic measures as harmful. Self-generated tests cut the resolved rate by 3.9 points because patch and test share the same error; the fix is a separately trained Test agent and a fail-closed harness. Trend Reader inference: "have the agent write tests" is not equivalent to the practice that works, and nothing in the evidence shows whether a different model as test author recovers most of the gain for a non-engineer.

4. Trust in a known agent configuration is what large projects substitute for review, and a solo builder has the opposite exposure. Grammel trusts a specific configuration with a track record over an unknown community agent; the individual is the unknown agent, running whatever the vendor updated this week, with no track record of their own configuration.

5. Reviewing the plan is both Orosz's cheapest approach and the documented attack surface. Orosz reports teams reviewing plans, tests or schemas instead of implementations, and thinks schema-only review is sensible for startups seeking product-market fit; the plan-injection result shows monitors of reasoning failing 25 to 33 percent of the time, with more monitor resources sometimes making it worse. The two are about different things (a human reading a plan versus a model monitoring a chain of thought), and no source tests a human reviewing an agent's plan against a planted one.

6. Delegation cost and return are measured on different sides of the same activity. Wentz treats the management burden as the reason agents have not spread; Tunguz reports Vercel putting an inbound agent's return at 32x with infrastructure in the single-digit thousands, in an interview where the same executive states a 99th-percentile-99-percent-of-the-time target (When Inbound Sells Itself). Neither figure includes the hours of the people who defined, connected and supervised the agent.

7. The one measured individual-scale failure rate is about configuration, not code quality. 170 of 1,645 apps failed on row-level security, an enforcement setting, not a logic bug. The checks the sources propose for individuals (authorization plan, schema, three enforcement points) match that failure mode, while the research-grade verification (execution-trace guards, trust scoring) targets agent behaviour at run time. Trend Reader inference: for a builder, the highest-yield check is a configuration audit, and no source has tested whether one would have caught the 170.

What no source has connected or measured

  • Oversight in hours. No source gives the per-task human minutes behind READY's review percentages, Duckbill's labels or OpenAI's approvals, which is the only unit a team of one can budget.
  • A price for the bought checks. The seven AI review vendors Orosz names are unpriced in the notes; HazardAuditor and AgentAudit are research artefacts; nothing states what any of them costs per repository or per month.
  • Whether risk triage works without a second person. Every triage scheme in the evidence routes high-risk changes to another human. The solo version would have to route them to a different agent or to a rule, and nobody tests that.
  • Whether the vibe-coder checklist closes the measured gap. The 1,645-app scan predates the series; no follow-up scan tests apps built with an authorization plan.
  • Whether the Sentry-style structural fix generalises below a team. Collapsing 200 pages into three templates took 2.5 developers two months; nobody reports the individual-scale equivalent.
  • The GCC or regulated-sector version. None of the eighteen notes discusses verification duties imposed by a regulator on a small team; READY's clinical case study is the closest and assumes an enterprise.

What the evidence supports

  1. The deployment question is reliability at a given oversight cost, not accuracy, and two systems within 0.3 accuracy points can differ by ten points of required human review.
  2. Blast radius is the shared triage rule at every scale: Duckbill's labels, Anthropic's and OpenAI's risk classification, Railway's "who is paged at 2am", and the survey's Turing-complete blast radius for agents.
  3. The checks a non-engineer can run are written before the agent builds and cost only time: an authorization plan, a fixed schema, enforcement at three points, and a rule for what is never shipped alone.
  4. The checks that reliably catch agent behaviour are trained systems (independent test agents, execution-trace guards, trace-level scoring), and the evidence contains no individual-scale version of any of them.
  5. Reducing human review in the cases that worked was paid for with automated gates (85 percent coverage, CI and performance harnesses, specialist review agents), so a builder without gates is carrying the review themselves whether or not they read the diff.

What would falsify the thesis

  • A READY-style profile published for a one-person workflow, with oversight measured in hours, showing the cheapest policy to be review rather than constraint.
  • A follow-up scan showing apps built with a written authorization plan failing row-level security at the same rate as the 170 of 1,645.
  • Evidence that a second model writing tests recovers most of ExecCritic's 11.4-point gain without role-specific post-training, which would put independent testing inside an individual's reach.
  • An AI review vendor publishing per-seat pricing and a measured defect-catch rate for solo repositories, which would move guard-style checks from bought systems to ordinary tools.
  • A large project reopening external contributions because its agent review became trustworthy, which would undercut the trust-in-known-configuration argument.
  • A case of risk triage working with agents rather than a second human on the high-risk branch.
